Um… well, technically I’m here because occupational health sent me. I mean… yes, I suppose I did. Not like some big dramatic plan at first, but I did start cutting things back on purpose. Most days now I just have coffee before the early shift, because it feels easier to get going that way and not have to think about food right away. Then at lunch I might have, like, half a protein bar—sometimes I tell myself I’ll eat the rest later, but I usually don’t.

And after work, if I do yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables afterward. That feels… manageable. Clean, I guess. If I eat more than that, the whole day starts to feel like it’s getting away from me, like I’ve lost control of it. So yes, I was trying to lose weight, or at least trying to keep things very controlled.

leaner and more disciplined.\n- key: S045\n  name: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\n  description: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.\n  manifestation: ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… I don’t like saying it that plainly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes, I’m afraid of it.

If the scale goes up even a little, even just a tiny amount, I get this immediate panic feeling, like everything I’ve been working so hard to keep controlled is slipping. It’s not just the number itself, it’s what I think it means. Like, if it goes up today, then maybe tomorrow it goes up more, and then I’m back to being heavy again, or I won’t be able to stop it. My mind goes straight there.

I know weight can fluctuate, people say that, but in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like proof that I’ve messed up somehow, or that I’ve gotten careless. And then I feel like I have to get control back quickly, because otherwise I’ll just… lose all of it. So yes, gaining weight really scares me.

I’m not sure what would count as “excessive,” exactly. I mean, compared to what I usually let myself eat, almost anything can feel like too much, if that makes sense. Like if I eat more than I planned, or if I finish something I only meant to have a bite of, my brain immediately labels it as too much.

But I don’t know if I’d say there are times where I eat a really large amount in a short period, like… I don’t think so? Not in the way I imagine you mean. It’s more that my idea of “too much” is probably not the same as someone else’s. Even a normal portion can feel excessive to me afterward, and then I replay it and get stuck on it.

So… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed myself eating a large amount all at once, but I do feel like I’ve overdone it even when it might not look that way from the outside.

I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. I usually feel like I’m trying very hard to stay in control the whole time, almost too much, honestly. Like I’m monitoring every bite and thinking about what it means, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had more than I meant to.

There are times where I eat something I hadn’t planned to eat, or I take a few more bites than I told myself I would, and then afterward I feel really upset, like I shouldn’t have done that. In my head it can feel like I “lost control,” but when I say that out loud, it’s not like I was eating and couldn’t stop myself for a long time or anything. It’s more… I broke one of my own rules, and then it feels huge.

So I guess I’m not sure. Emotionally, yes, it can feel out of control if I go off what I planned. But actually eating in a way where I’m not aware or can’t stop—no, I don’t think that really happens.

I work on the floor, so it’s physical, you know, standing, moving parts, keeping up with the line, and they seemed worried about that.\n\nI guess I didn’t really see it the same way. I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, like I had better control over myself. I know they made it sound like this urgent thing, but to me it felt more like I was being careful and focused. But they were pretty insistent that I come in and talk to someone, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Did you intentionally try to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose I did. Not like some big dramatic plan at first, but I did start cutting things back on purpose. Most days now I just have coffee before the early shift, because it feels easier to get going that way and not have to think about food right away. Then at lunch I might have, like, half a protein bar—sometimes I tell myself I’ll eat the rest later, but I usually don’t.\n\nAnd after work, if I do yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables afterward. That feels… manageable. Clean, I guess. If I eat more than that, the whole day starts to feel like it’s getting away from me, like I’ve lost control of it. So yes, I was trying to lose weight, or at least trying to keep things very controlled.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t like saying it that plainly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little, even just a tiny amount, I get this immediate panic feeling, like everything I’ve been working so hard to keep controlled is slipping. It’s not just the number itself, it’s what I think it means. Like, if it goes up today, then maybe tomorrow it goes up more, and then I’m back to being heavy again, or I won’t be able to stop it. My mind goes straight there.\n\nI know weight can fluctuate, people say that, but in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like proof that I’ve messed up somehow, or that I’ve gotten careless. And then I feel like I have to get control back quickly, because otherwise I’ll just… lose all of it. So yes, gaining weight really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what would count as “excessive,” exactly. I mean, compared to what I usually let myself eat, almost anything can feel like too much, if that makes sense. Like if I eat more than I planned, or if I finish something I only meant to have a bite of, my brain immediately labels it as too much.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there are times where I eat a really large amount in a short period, like… I don’t think so? Not in the way I imagine you mean. It’s more that my idea of “too much” is probably not the same as someone else’s. Even a normal portion can feel excessive to me afterward, and then I replay it and get stuck on it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed myself eating a large amount all at once, but I do feel like I’ve overdone it even when it might not look that way from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. I usually feel like I’m trying very hard to stay in control the whole time, almost too much, honestly. Like I’m monitoring every bite and thinking about what it means, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had more than I meant to.\n\nThere are times where I eat something I hadn’t planned to eat, or I take a few more bites than I told myself I would, and then afterward I feel really upset, like I shouldn’t have done that. In my head it can feel like I “lost control,” but when I say that out loud, it’s not like I was eating and couldn’t stop myself for a long time or anything. It’s more… I broke one of my own rules, and then it feels huge.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. Emotionally, yes, it can feel out of control if I go off what I planned. But actually eating in a way where I’m not aware or can’t stop—no, I don’t think that really happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. I mean, that’s hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s “a lot” is probably really distorted. I can feel like I ate some enormous amount when, if someone else looked at it, they might say it was just a normal amount or even not much at all.

But if you mean objectively, like eating much more than most people would in the same situation, in a short time… no, I don’t think that really happens. I don’t have examples where I could say, yes, that was clearly a lot compared to other people. It’s more that afterward I can feel like it was too much because it was more than I planned, or because it didn’t fit whatever rule I had in my head.

So I guess my honest answer is no, not really—not that I’ve noticed. It just feels excessive to me sometimes, even when I’m not sure it actually is.

It’s more… I broke one of my own rules, and then it feels huge.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. Emotionally, yes, it can feel out of control if I go off what I planned. But actually eating in a way where I’m not aware or can’t stop—no, I don’t think that really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that is much larger than most people would eat in a similar time period under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, that’s hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s “a lot” is probably really distorted. I can feel like I ate some enormous amount when, if someone else looked at it, they might say it was just a normal amount or even not much at all.\n\nBut if you mean objectively, like eating much more than most people would in the same situation, in a short time… no, I don’t think that really happens. I don’t have examples where I could say, yes, that was clearly a lot compared to other people. It’s more that afterward I can feel like it was too much because it was more than I planned, or because it didn’t fit whatever rule I had in my head.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is no, not really—not that I’ve noticed. It just feels excessive to me sometimes, even when I’m not sure it actually is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I have episodes like that, no. At least not the way you’re describing it, where it’s a large amount of food and I can’t stop once I’ve started.

I can get really distressed if I eat more than I meant to, even if it’s not much by someone else’s standards. Like, I might have something and then think, “Why did I do that?” or feel like I should’ve stopped sooner. But I’m still aware of what I’m doing, and I’m not eating huge amounts in a way where I feel completely unable to stop.

It’s more like… the feeling of losing control can happen in my head because I broke whatever rule I had for myself. But if you’re asking whether I have actual episodes of eating a lot of food and feeling physically or mentally unable to stop, I don’t think so. That doesn’t really fit what happens for me.

They said my weight is, their word, “dangerously low,” especially for the kind of work I do, running presses and doing fabrication all day. I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, like I had better control over myself. I know they made it sound like this urgent thing, but to me it felt more like I was being careful and focused. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… no, I don’t think I do that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after eating before, like really panicky or guilty or like I wish I could undo it, but I don’t make myself throw up.

Sometimes I worry that if I say I feel that distressed, people assume that’s what I’m doing, but it’s not. I might sit with the feeling for a long time, or replay what I ate in my head, but I’m not going to the bathroom to make myself vomit or anything like that.

So… no. Not on purpose.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, technically I’m here because occupational health sent me. They said my weight is, their word, “dangerously low,” especially for the kind of work I do, running presses and doing fabrication all day. I work on the floor, so it’s physical, you know, standing, moving parts, keeping up with the line, and they seemed worried about that.\n\nI guess I didn’t really see it the same way. I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, like I had better control over myself. I know they made it sound like this urgent thing, but to me it felt more like I was being careful and focused. But they were pretty insistent that I come in and talk to someone, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Did you intentionally try to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose I did. Not like some big dramatic plan at first, but I did start cutting things back on purpose. Most days now I just have coffee before the early shift, because it feels easier to get going that way and not have to think about food right away. Then at lunch I might have, like, half a protein bar—sometimes I tell myself I’ll eat the rest later, but I usually don’t.\n\nAnd after work, if I do yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables afterward. That feels… manageable. Clean, I guess. If I eat more than that, the whole day starts to feel like it’s getting away from me, like I’ve lost control of it. I mean… I don’t like saying it that plainly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little, even just a tiny amount, I get this immediate panic feeling, like everything I’ve been working so hard to keep controlled is slipping. It’s not just the number itself, it’s what I think it means. Like, if it goes up today, then maybe tomorrow it goes up more, and then I’m back to being heavy again, or I won’t be able to stop it. I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of it in such a straightforward way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to burn it off.” I don’t usually calculate it like that out loud or in some exact way.

I mean… I do think about my body and what I’ve eaten, and I can get uncomfortable with the idea of food just sort of “sitting there,” if that makes sense. But as far as exercise specifically to make up for eating, I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear routine where every time I eat something I go exercise because of it.

Sometimes I probably move around more when I’m feeling anxious about things, but I’m not sure I’d call that exercise in the way you mean. I guess I’d need to understand what you count as exercise—like formal workouts, or just trying to be active, or not wanting to sit still after eating.

So… I don’t know that I can say yes. It’s not something I’ve really labeled that way.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, technically I’m here because occupational health sent me. They said my weight is, their word, “dangerously low,” especially for the kind of work I do, running presses and doing fabrication all day. I work on the floor, so it’s physical, you know, standing, moving parts, keeping up with the line, and they seemed worried about that.\n\nI guess I didn’t really see it the same way. I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, like I had better control over myself. I know they made it sound like this urgent thing, but to me it felt more like I was being careful and focused. But they were pretty insistent that I come in and talk to someone, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Did you intentionally try to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose I did. Not like some big dramatic plan at first, but I did start cutting things back on purpose. Most days now I just have coffee before the early shift, because it feels easier to get going that way and not have to think about food right away. Then at lunch I might have, like, half a protein bar—sometimes I tell myself I’ll eat the rest later, but I usually don’t.\n\nAnd after work, if I do yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables afterward. That feels… manageable. Clean, I guess. If I eat more than that, the whole day starts to feel like it’s getting away from me, like I’ve lost control of it. So yes, I was trying to lose weight, or at least trying to keep things very controlled.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t like saying it that plainly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little, even just a tiny amount, I get this immediate panic feeling, like everything I’ve been working so hard to keep controlled is slipping. It’s not just the number itself, it’s what I think it means. Like, if it goes up today, then maybe tomorrow it goes up more, and then I’m back to being heavy again, or I won’t be able to stop it. My mind goes straight there.\n\nI know weight can fluctuate, people say that, but in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like proof that I’ve messed up somehow, or that I’ve gotten careless. And then I feel like I have to get control back quickly, because otherwise I’ll just… lose all of it. So yes, gaining weight really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what would count as “excessive,” exactly. I mean, compared to what I usually let myself eat, almost anything can feel like too much, if that makes sense. Like if I eat more than I planned, or if I finish something I only meant to have a bite of, my brain immediately labels it as too much.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there are times where I eat a really large amount in a short period, like… I don’t think so? Not in the way I imagine you mean. It’s more that my idea of “too much” is probably not the same as someone else’s. Even a normal portion can feel excessive to me afterward, and then I replay it and get stuck on it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed myself eating a large amount all at once, but I do feel like I’ve overdone it even when it might not look that way from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. I usually feel like I’m trying very hard to stay in control the whole time, almost too much, honestly. Like I’m monitoring every bite and thinking about what it means, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had more than I meant to.\n\nThere are times where I eat something I hadn’t planned to eat, or I take a few more bites than I told myself I would, and then afterward I feel really upset, like I shouldn’t have done that. In my head it can feel like I “lost control,” but when I say that out loud, it’s not like I was eating and couldn’t stop myself for a long time or anything. It’s more… I broke one of my own rules, and then it feels huge.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. Emotionally, yes, it can feel out of control if I go off what I planned. But actually eating in a way where I’m not aware or can’t stop—no, I don’t think that really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that is much larger than most people would eat in a similar time period under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, that’s hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s “a lot” is probably really distorted. I can feel like I ate some enormous amount when, if someone else looked at it, they might say it was just a normal amount or even not much at all.\n\nBut if you mean objectively, like eating much more than most people would in the same situation, in a short time… no, I don’t think that really happens. But I’m still aware of what I’m doing, and I’m not eating huge amounts in a way where I feel completely unable to stop.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of losing control can happen in my head because I broke whatever rule I had for myself. But if you’re asking whether I have actual episodes of eating a lot of food and feeling physically or mentally unable to stop, I don’t think so. That doesn’t really fit what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I do that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after eating before, like really panicky or guilty or like I wish I could undo it, but I don’t make myself throw up.\n\nSometimes I worry that if I say I feel that distressed, people assume that’s what I’m doing, but it’s not. I might sit with the feeling for a long time, or replay what I ate in my head, but I’m not going to the bathroom to make myself vomit or anything like that.\n\nSo… no. Not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or to make up for eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of it in such a straightforward way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to burn it off.” I don’t usually calculate it like that out loud or in some exact way.\n\nI mean… I do think about my body and what I’ve eaten, and I can get uncomfortable with the idea of food just sort of “sitting there,” if that makes sense. But as far as exercise specifically to make up for eating, I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear routine where every time I eat something I go exercise because of it.\n\nSometimes I probably move around more when I’m feeling anxious about things, but I’m not sure I’d call that exercise in the way you mean. I guess I’d need to understand what you count as exercise—like formal workouts, or just trying to be active, or not wanting to sit still after eating.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say yes. It’s not something I’ve really labeled that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t really know how to answer that one. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—I’m not totally sure what counts, like whether you mean actual pills or certain drinks or things people take for water weight.

I don’t think that’s something I do in a deliberate way to “counteract” eating. Like, I don’t have a routine where I eat and then take something because of it. I’d probably be nervous to take stuff like that anyway, because I wouldn’t really know what it was doing to my body.

I mean, I can get really preoccupied after eating and feel like I need to somehow fix it, but in terms of laxatives or water pills specifically, no, not that I can think of. If there’s something broader you mean by that, you might have to explain it a little, but I don’t think so.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, technically I’m here because occupational health sent me. They said my weight is, their word, “dangerously low,” especially for the kind of work I do, running presses and doing fabrication all day. I work on the floor, so it’s physical, you know, standing, moving parts, keeping up with the line, and they seemed worried about that.\n\nI guess I didn’t really see it the same way. I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, like I had better control over myself. I know they made it sound like this urgent thing, but to me it felt more like I was being careful and focused. But they were pretty insistent that I come in and talk to someone, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Did you intentionally try to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose I did. Not like some big dramatic plan at first, but I did start cutting things back on purpose. Most days now I just have coffee before the early shift, because it feels easier to get going that way and not have to think about food right away. Then at lunch I might have, like, half a protein bar—sometimes I tell myself I’ll eat the rest later, but I usually don’t.\n\nAnd after work, if I do yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables afterward. That feels… manageable. Clean, I guess. If I eat more than that, the whole day starts to feel like it’s getting away from me, like I’ve lost control of it. So yes, I was trying to lose weight, or at least trying to keep things very controlled.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t like saying it that plainly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little, even just a tiny amount, I get this immediate panic feeling, like everything I’ve been working so hard to keep controlled is slipping. It’s not just the number itself, it’s what I think it means. Like, if it goes up today, then maybe tomorrow it goes up more, and then I’m back to being heavy again, or I won’t be able to stop it. My mind goes straight there.\n\nI know weight can fluctuate, people say that, but in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like proof that I’ve messed up somehow, or that I’ve gotten careless. And then I feel like I have to get control back quickly, because otherwise I’ll just… lose all of it. So yes, gaining weight really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what would count as “excessive,” exactly. I mean, compared to what I usually let myself eat, almost anything can feel like too much, if that makes sense. Like if I eat more than I planned, or if I finish something I only meant to have a bite of, my brain immediately labels it as too much.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there are times where I eat a really large amount in a short period, like… I don’t think so? Not in the way I imagine you mean. It’s more that my idea of “too much” is probably not the same as someone else’s. Even a normal portion can feel excessive to me afterward, and then I replay it and get stuck on it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed myself eating a large amount all at once, but I do feel like I’ve overdone it even when it might not look that way from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. I usually feel like I’m trying very hard to stay in control the whole time, almost too much, honestly. Like I’m monitoring every bite and thinking about what it means, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had more than I meant to.\n\nThere are times where I eat something I hadn’t planned to eat, or I take a few more bites than I told myself I would, and then afterward I feel really upset, like I shouldn’t have done that. In my head it can feel like I “lost control,” but when I say that out loud, it’s not like I was eating and couldn’t stop myself for a long time or anything. It’s more… I broke one of my own rules, and then it feels huge.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. Emotionally, yes, it can feel out of control if I go off what I planned. But actually eating in a way where I’m not aware or can’t stop—no, I don’t think that really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that is much larger than most people would eat in a similar time period under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, that’s hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s “a lot” is probably really distorted. I can feel like I ate some enormous amount when, if someone else looked at it, they might say it was just a normal amount or even not much at all.\n\nBut if you mean objectively, like eating much more than most people would in the same situation, in a short time… no, I don’t think that really happens. I don’t have examples where I could say, yes, that was clearly a lot compared to other people. It just feels excessive to me sometimes, even when I’m not sure it actually is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have episodes like that, no. At least not the way you’re describing it, where it’s a large amount of food and I can’t stop once I’ve started.\n\nI can get really distressed if I eat more than I meant to, even if it’s not much by someone else’s standards. Even a normal portion can feel excessive to me afterward, and then I replay it and get stuck on it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed myself eating a large amount all at once, but I do feel like I’ve overdone it even when it might not look that way from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. I usually feel like I’m trying very hard to stay in control the whole time, almost too much, honestly. Like I’m monitoring every bite and thinking about what it means, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had more than I meant to.\n\nThere are times where I eat something I hadn’t planned to eat, or I take a few more bites than I told myself I would, and then afterward I feel really upset, like I shouldn’t have done that. In my head it can feel like I “lost control,” but when I say that out loud, it’s not like I was eating and couldn’t stop myself for a long time or anything. It’s more… I broke one of my own rules, and then it feels huge.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. Emotionally, yes, it can feel out of control if I go off what I planned. But actually eating in a way where I’m not aware or can’t stop—no, I don’t think that really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that is much larger than most people would eat in a similar time period under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, that’s hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s “a lot” is probably really distorted. I can feel like I ate some enormous amount when, if someone else looked at it, they might say it was just a normal amount or even not much at all.\n\nBut if you mean objectively, like eating much more than most people would in the same situation, in a short time… no, I don’t think that really happens. I don’t have examples where I could say, yes, that was clearly a lot compared to other people. It’s more that afterward I can feel like it was too much because it was more than I planned, or because it didn’t fit whatever rule I had in my head.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is no, not really—not that I’ve noticed. It just feels excessive to me sometimes, even when I’m not sure it actually is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have episodes like that, no. At least not the way you’re describing it, where it’s a large amount of food and I can’t stop once I’ve started.\n\nI can get really distressed if I eat more than I meant to, even if it’s not much by someone else’s standards. Like, I might have something and then think, “Why did I do that?” or feel like I should’ve stopped sooner. But I’m still aware of what I’m doing, and I’m not eating huge amounts in a way where I feel completely unable to stop.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of losing control can happen in my head because I broke whatever rule I had for myself. But if you’re asking whether I have actual episodes of eating a lot of food and feeling physically or mentally unable to stop, I don’t think so. That doesn’t really fit what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I do that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after eating before, like really panicky or guilty or like I wish I could undo it, but I don’t make myself throw up.\n\nSometimes I worry that if I say I feel that distressed, people assume that’s what I’m doing, but it’s not. I might sit with the feeling for a long time, or replay what I ate in my head, but I’m not going to the bathroom to make myself vomit or anything like that.\n\nSo… no. Not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or to make up for eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of it in such a straightforward way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to burn it off.” I don’t usually calculate it like that out loud or in some exact way.\n\nI mean… I do think about my body and what I’ve eaten, and I can get uncomfortable with the idea of food just sort of “sitting there,” if that makes sense. But as far as exercise specifically to make up for eating, I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear routine where every time I eat something I go exercise because of it.\n\nSometimes I probably move around more when I’m feeling anxious about things, but I’m not sure I’d call that exercise in the way you mean. I guess I’d need to understand what you count as exercise—like formal workouts, or just trying to be active, or not wanting to sit still after eating.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say yes. It’s not something I’ve really labeled that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to lose weight or counteract the effects of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—I’m not totally sure what counts, like whether you mean actual pills or certain drinks or things people take for water weight.\n\nI don’t think that’s something I do in a deliberate way to “counteract” eating. Like, I don’t have a routine where I eat and then take something because of it. I’d probably be nervous to take stuff like that anyway, because I wouldn’t really know what it was doing to my body.\n\nI mean, I can get really preoccupied after eating and feel like I need to somehow fix it, but in terms of laxatives or water pills specifically, no, not that I can think of. If there’s something broader you mean by that, you might have to explain it a little, but I don’t think so.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes… it does. Probably more than I like admitting.

If the scale goes up even a little, I panic. Like, it doesn’t feel like just a number changing — it feels like proof that I’m losing control, and then my mind goes straight to, “This is the beginning of everything falling apart.” I know that might sound dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like all the control I’ve worked so hard to keep could just slip away, and I’ll end up heavy again.

And then it kind of follows me around the rest of the day. I’m distracted, I’m irritable, I don’t want to be around people, because I’m stuck in my head thinking about what happened and what it means. Even normal things feel harder because I’m checking myself, thinking about my body, wondering if people can tell. So yes, it affects things. I try to act like it doesn’t, but it does.

Like if I eat more than I planned, or if I finish something I only meant to have a bite of, my brain immediately labels it as too much.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there are times where I eat a really large amount in a short period, like… I don’t think so? Even a normal portion can feel excessive to me afterward, and then I replay it and get stuck on it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed myself eating a large amount all at once, but I do feel like I’ve overdone it even when it might not look that way from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. I usually feel like I’m trying very hard to stay in control the whole time, almost too much, honestly. Like I’m monitoring every bite and thinking about what it means, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had more than I meant to.\n\nThere are times where I eat something I hadn’t planned to eat, or I take a few more bites than I told myself I would, and then afterward I feel really upset, like I shouldn’t have done that. In my head it can feel like I “lost control,” but when I say that out loud, it’s not like I was eating and couldn’t stop myself for a long time or anything. It’s more… I broke one of my own rules, and then it feels huge.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. Emotionally, yes, it can feel out of control if I go off what I planned. I mean, that’s hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s “a lot” is probably really distorted. I can feel like I ate some enormous amount when, if someone else looked at it, they might say it was just a normal amount or even not much at all.\n\nBut if you mean objectively, like eating much more than most people would in the same situation, in a short time… no, I don’t think that really happens. I don’t have examples where I could say, yes, that was clearly a lot compared to other people. It’s more that afterward I can feel like it was too much because it was more than I planned, or because it didn’t fit whatever rule I had in my head.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is no, not really—not that I’ve noticed. It just feels excessive to me sometimes, even when I’m not sure it actually is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have episodes like that, no. At least not the way you’re describing it, where it’s a large amount of food and I can’t stop once I’ve started.\n\nI can get really distressed if I eat more than I meant to, even if it’s not much by someone else’s standards. Like, I might have something and then think, “Why did I do that?” or feel like I should’ve stopped sooner. But I’m still aware of what I’m doing, and I’m not eating huge amounts in a way where I feel completely unable to stop.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of losing control can happen in my head because I broke whatever rule I had for myself. But if you’re asking whether I have actual episodes of eating a lot of food and feeling physically or mentally unable to stop, I don’t think so. That doesn’t really fit what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I do that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after eating before, like really panicky or guilty or like I wish I could undo it, but I don’t make myself throw up.\n\nSometimes I worry that if I say I feel that distressed, people assume that’s what I’m doing, but it’s not. I might sit with the feeling for a long time, or replay what I ate in my head, but I’m not going to the bathroom to make myself vomit or anything like that.\n\nSo… no. Not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or to make up for eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of it in such a straightforward way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to burn it off.” I don’t usually calculate it like that out loud or in some exact way.\n\nI mean… I do think about my body and what I’ve eaten, and I can get uncomfortable with the idea of food just sort of “sitting there,” if that makes sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It’s definitely caused problems with work, and then that sort of spills over into home.

At work, occupational health got involved because of my weight, and ever since they flagged it, things have been… complicated. I do metal and plastics work, and there are certain press jobs they haven’t wanted me doing, or they’ve kept me off some of that work, because they’re worried about me. And I understand they have rules and safety concerns, but it’s also embarrassing and frustrating because I feel like people are watching me or deciding what I can and can’t handle.

They’ve also been pushing me to get urgent care, like it can’t wait, and that’s disrupted my shifts. It’s not just a small inconvenience — if I miss time or get moved around, it affects my pay, and then at home I’m trying to figure out bills and how to keep everything covered. So then I’m stressed about money on top of everything else.

With family and friends, I think it creates tension because they can tell something is going on, and I don’t always want to talk about it. But the work part is the clearest problem right now — being flagged, being taken off some press work, and having my schedule messed with has made it harder to keep up like I normally would.

Like if I eat more than I planned, or if I finish something I only meant to have a bite of, my brain immediately labels it as too much.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there are times where I eat a really large amount in a short period, like… I don’t think so? Not in the way I imagine you mean. It’s more that my idea of “too much” is probably not the same as someone else’s. Even a normal portion can feel excessive to me afterward, and then I replay it and get stuck on it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed myself eating a large amount all at once, but I do feel like I’ve overdone it even when it might not look that way from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. I usually feel like I’m trying very hard to stay in control the whole time, almost too much, honestly. Like I’m monitoring every bite and thinking about what it means, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had more than I meant to.\n\nThere are times where I eat something I hadn’t planned to eat, or I take a few more bites than I told myself I would, and then afterward I feel really upset, like I shouldn’t have done that. In my head it can feel like I “lost control,” but when I say that out loud, it’s not like I was eating and couldn’t stop myself for a long time or anything. It’s more… I broke one of my own rules, and then it feels huge.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. Emotionally, yes, it can feel out of control if I go off what I planned. But actually eating in a way where I’m not aware or can’t stop—no, I don’t think that really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that is much larger than most people would eat in a similar time period under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, that’s hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s “a lot” is probably really distorted. I can feel like I ate some enormous amount when, if someone else looked at it, they might say it was just a normal amount or even not much at all.\n\nBut if you mean objectively, like eating much more than most people would in the same situation, in a short time… no, I don’t think that really happens. I don’t have examples where I could say, yes, that was clearly a lot compared to other people. It’s more that afterward I can feel like it was too much because it was more than I planned, or because it didn’t fit whatever rule I had in my head.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is no, not really—not that I’ve noticed. It just feels excessive to me sometimes, even when I’m not sure it actually is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have episodes like that, no. At least not the way you’re describing it, where it’s a large amount of food and I can’t stop once I’ve started.\n\nI can get really distressed if I eat more than I meant to, even if it’s not much by someone else’s standards. Like, I might have something and then think, “Why did I do that?” or feel like I should’ve stopped sooner. But I’m still aware of what I’m doing, and I’m not eating huge amounts in a way where I feel completely unable to stop.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of losing control can happen in my head because I broke whatever rule I had for myself. But if you’re asking whether I have actual episodes of eating a lot of food and feeling physically or mentally unable to stop, I don’t think so. That doesn’t really fit what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I do that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after eating before, like really panicky or guilty or like I wish I could undo it, but I don’t make myself throw up.\n\nSometimes I worry that if I say I feel that distressed, people assume that’s what I’m doing, but it’s not. I might sit with the feeling for a long time, or replay what I ate in my head, but I’m not going to the bathroom to make myself vomit or anything like that.\n\nSo… no. Not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or to make up for eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of it in such a straightforward way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to burn it off.” I don’t usually calculate it like that out loud or in some exact way.\n\nI mean… I do think about my body and what I’ve eaten, and I can get uncomfortable with the idea of food just sort of “sitting there,” if that makes sense. But as far as exercise specifically to make up for eating, I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear routine where every time I eat something I go exercise because of it.\n\nSometimes I probably move around more when I’m feeling anxious about things, but I’m not sure I’d call that exercise in the way you mean. I guess I’d need to understand what you count as exercise—like formal workouts, or just trying to be active, or not wanting to sit still after eating.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say yes. It’s not something I’ve really labeled that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to lose weight or counteract the effects of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—I’m not totally sure what counts, like whether you mean actual pills or certain drinks or things people take for water weight.\n\nI don’t think that’s something I do in a deliberate way to “counteract” eating. Like, I don’t have a routine where I eat and then take something because of it. I’d probably be nervous to take stuff like that anyway, because I wouldn’t really know what it was doing to my body.\n\nI mean, I can get really preoccupied after eating and feel like I need to somehow fix it, but in terms of laxatives or water pills specifically, no, not that I can think of. If there’s something broader you mean by that, you might have to explain it a little, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your daily activities or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does. Probably more than I like admitting.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little, I panic. Like, it doesn’t feel like just a number changing — it feels like proof that I’m losing control, and then my mind goes straight to, “This is the beginning of everything falling apart.” I know that might sound dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like all the control I’ve worked so hard to keep could just slip away, and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nAnd then it kind of follows me around the rest of the day. I’m distracted, I’m irritable, I don’t want to be around people, because I’m stuck in my head thinking about what happened and what it means. Even normal things feel harder because I’m checking myself, thinking about my body, wondering if people can tell. So yes, it affects things. I try to act like it doesn’t, but it does.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight or your eating habits caused you any problems at work or in your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely caused problems with work, and then that sort of spills over into home.\n\nAt work, occupational health got involved because of my weight, and ever since they flagged it, things have been… complicated. I do metal and plastics work, and there are certain press jobs they haven’t wanted me doing, or they’ve kept me off some of that work, because they’re worried about me. And I understand they have rules and safety concerns, but it’s also embarrassing and frustrating because I feel like people are watching me or deciding what I can and can’t handle.\n\nThey’ve also been pushing me to get urgent care, like it can’t wait, and that’s disrupted my shifts. It’s not just a small inconvenience — if I miss time or get moved around, it affects my pay, and then at home I’m trying to figure out bills and how to keep everything covered. So then I’m stressed about money on top of everything else.\n\nWith family and friends, I think it creates tension because they can tell something is going on, and I don’t always want to talk about it. But the work part is the clearest problem right now — being flagged, being taken off some press work, and having my schedule messed with has made it harder to keep up like I normally would.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

If there’s something broader you mean by that, you might have to explain it a little, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your daily activities or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does. Probably more than I like admitting.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little, I panic. Like, it doesn’t feel like just a number changing — it feels like proof that I’m losing control, and then my mind goes straight to, “This is the beginning of everything falling apart.” I know that might sound dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like all the control I’ve worked so hard to keep could just slip away, and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nAnd then it kind of follows me around the rest of the day. I’m distracted, I’m irritable, I don’t want to be around people, because I’m stuck in my head thinking about what happened and what it means. Even normal things feel harder because I’m checking myself, thinking about my body, wondering if people can tell. So yes, it affects things. I try to act like it doesn’t, but it does.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight or your eating habits caused you any problems at work or in your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely caused problems with work, and then that sort of spills over into home.\n\nAt work, occupational health got involved because of my weight, and ever since they flagged it, things have been… complicated. I do metal and plastics work, and there are certain press jobs they haven’t wanted me doing, or they’ve kept me off some of that work, because they’re worried about me. And I understand they have rules and safety concerns, but it’s also embarrassing and frustrating because I feel like people are watching me or deciding what I can and can’t handle.\n\nThey’ve also been pushing me to get urgent care, like it can’t wait, and that’s disrupted my shifts. It’s not just a small inconvenience — if I miss time or get moved around, it affects my pay, and then at home I’m trying to figure out bills and how to keep everything covered. So then I’m stressed about money on top of everything else.\n\nWith family and friends, I think it creates tension because they can tell something is going on, and I don’t always want to talk about it. But the work part is the clearest problem right now — being flagged, being taken off some press work, and having my schedule messed with has made it harder to keep up like I normally would.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you regularly have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 45 / metal_worker_or_plastic_worker / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. Like, I wouldn’t say I have episodes where I’m eating a huge amount all at once and can’t stop, if that’s what you’re asking.

Sometimes I get confused with that question, because what feels like “a lot” to me might not actually be a lot to someone else. I can feel really uncomfortable even after what other people would probably consider a normal amount, so in my head it can feel like I’ve overeaten. But if you mean objectively, like a large amount of food in a short period, then no, I don’t regularly do that.

I’m more likely to be thinking too much about what I’ve had than eating large amounts quickly, if that makes sense.

---------- turn 13 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, technically I’m here because occupational health sent me. They said my weight is, their word, “dangerously low,” especially for the kind of work I do, running presses and doing fabrication all day. I work on the floor, so it’s physical, you know, standing, moving parts, keeping up with the line, and they seemed worried about that.\n\nI guess I didn’t really see it the same way. I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, like I had better control over myself. I know they made it sound like this urgent thing, but to me it felt more like I was being careful and focused. But they were pretty insistent that I come in and talk to someone, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Did you intentionally try to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose I did. Not like some big dramatic plan at first, but I did start cutting things back on purpose. Most days now I just have coffee before the early shift, because it feels easier to get going that way and not have to think about food right away. Then at lunch I might have, like, half a protein bar—sometimes I tell myself I’ll eat the rest later, but I usually don’t.\n\nAnd after work, if I do yoga, I’ll have a small bowl of vegetables afterward. That feels… manageable. Clean, I guess. If I eat more than that, the whole day starts to feel like it’s getting away from me, like I’ve lost control of it. So yes, I was trying to lose weight, or at least trying to keep things very controlled.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t like saying it that plainly, because it sounds irrational when I hear it out loud, but yes, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little, even just a tiny amount, I get this immediate panic feeling, like everything I’ve been working so hard to keep controlled is slipping. It’s not just the number itself, it’s what I think it means. Like, if it goes up today, then maybe tomorrow it goes up more, and then I’m back to being heavy again, or I won’t be able to stop it. My mind goes straight there.\n\nI know weight can fluctuate, people say that, but in the moment it doesn’t feel harmless. It feels like proof that I’ve messed up somehow, or that I’ve gotten careless. And then I feel like I have to get control back quickly, because otherwise I’ll just… lose all of it. So yes, gaining weight really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what would count as “excessive,” exactly. I mean, compared to what I usually let myself eat, almost anything can feel like too much, if that makes sense. Like if I eat more than I planned, or if I finish something I only meant to have a bite of, my brain immediately labels it as too much.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there are times where I eat a really large amount in a short period, like… I don’t think so? Not in the way I imagine you mean. It’s more that my idea of “too much” is probably not the same as someone else’s. Even a normal portion can feel excessive to me afterward, and then I replay it and get stuck on it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed myself eating a large amount all at once, but I do feel like I’ve overdone it even when it might not look that way from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. I usually feel like I’m trying very hard to stay in control the whole time, almost too much, honestly. Like I’m monitoring every bite and thinking about what it means, whether I should stop, whether I’ve already had more than I meant to.\n\nThere are times where I eat something I hadn’t planned to eat, or I take a few more bites than I told myself I would, and then afterward I feel really upset, like I shouldn’t have done that. In my head it can feel like I “lost control,” but when I say that out loud, it’s not like I was eating and couldn’t stop myself for a long time or anything. It’s more… I broke one of my own rules, and then it feels huge.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure. Emotionally, yes, it can feel out of control if I go off what I planned. But actually eating in a way where I’m not aware or can’t stop—no, I don’t think that really happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that is much larger than most people would eat in a similar time period under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, that’s hard for me to judge, because my sense of what’s “a lot” is probably really distorted. I can feel like I ate some enormous amount when, if someone else looked at it, they might say it was just a normal amount or even not much at all.\n\nBut if you mean objectively, like eating much more than most people would in the same situation, in a short time… no, I don’t think that really happens. I don’t have examples where I could say, yes, that was clearly a lot compared to other people. It’s more that afterward I can feel like it was too much because it was more than I planned, or because it didn’t fit whatever rule I had in my head.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is no, not really—not that I’ve noticed. It just feels excessive to me sometimes, even when I’m not sure it actually is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have episodes like that, no. At least not the way you’re describing it, where it’s a large amount of food and I can’t stop once I’ve started.\n\nI can get really distressed if I eat more than I meant to, even if it’s not much by someone else’s standards. Like, I might have something and then think, “Why did I do that?” or feel like I should’ve stopped sooner. But I’m still aware of what I’m doing, and I’m not eating huge amounts in a way where I feel completely unable to stop.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of losing control can happen in my head because I broke whatever rule I had for myself. But if you’re asking whether I have actual episodes of eating a lot of food and feeling physically or mentally unable to stop, I don’t think so. That doesn’t really fit what happens for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I do that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after eating before, like really panicky or guilty or like I wish I could undo it, but I don’t make myself throw up.\n\nSometimes I worry that if I say I feel that distressed, people assume that’s what I’m doing, but it’s not. I might sit with the feeling for a long time, or replay what I ate in my head, but I’m not going to the bathroom to make myself vomit or anything like that.\n\nSo… no. Not on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or to make up for eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of it in such a straightforward way, like, “I ate this, so now I have to burn it off.” I don’t usually calculate it like that out loud or in some exact way.\n\nI mean… I do think about my body and what I’ve eaten, and I can get uncomfortable with the idea of food just sort of “sitting there,” if that makes sense. But as far as exercise specifically to make up for eating, I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear routine where every time I eat something I go exercise because of it.\n\nSometimes I probably move around more when I’m feeling anxious about things, but I’m not sure I’d call that exercise in the way you mean. I guess I’d need to understand what you count as exercise—like formal workouts, or just trying to be active, or not wanting to sit still after eating.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say yes. It’s not something I’ve really labeled that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to lose weight or counteract the effects of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—I’m not totally sure what counts, like whether you mean actual pills or certain drinks or things people take for water weight.\n\nI don’t think that’s something I do in a deliberate way to “counteract” eating. Like, I don’t have a routine where I eat and then take something because of it. I’d probably be nervous to take stuff like that anyway, because I wouldn’t really know what it was doing to my body.\n\nI mean, I can get really preoccupied after eating and feel like I need to somehow fix it, but in terms of laxatives or water pills specifically, no, not that I can think of. If there’s something broader you mean by that, you might have to explain it a little, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your daily activities or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does. Probably more than I like admitting.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little, I panic. Like, it doesn’t feel like just a number changing — it feels like proof that I’m losing control, and then my mind goes straight to, “This is the beginning of everything falling apart.” I know that might sound dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like all the control I’ve worked so hard to keep could just slip away, and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nAnd then it kind of follows me around the rest of the day. I’m distracted, I’m irritable, I don’t want to be around people, because I’m stuck in my head thinking about what happened and what it means. Even normal things feel harder because I’m checking myself, thinking about my body, wondering if people can tell. So yes, it affects things. I try to act like it doesn’t, but it does.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight or your eating habits caused you any problems at work or in your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely caused problems with work, and then that sort of spills over into home.\n\nAt work, occupational health got involved because of my weight, and ever since they flagged it, things have been… complicated. I do metal and plastics work, and there are certain press jobs they haven’t wanted me doing, or they’ve kept me off some of that work, because they’re worried about me. And I understand they have rules and safety concerns, but it’s also embarrassing and frustrating because I feel like people are watching me or deciding what I can and can’t handle.\n\nThey’ve also been pushing me to get urgent care, like it can’t wait, and that’s disrupted my shifts. It’s not just a small inconvenience — if I miss time or get moved around, it affects my pay, and then at home I’m trying to figure out bills and how to keep everything covered. So then I’m stressed about money on top of everything else.\n\nWith family and friends, I think it creates tension because they can tell something is going on, and I don’t always want to talk about it. But the work part is the clearest problem right now — being flagged, being taken off some press work, and having my schedule messed with has made it harder to keep up like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you regularly have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. Like, I wouldn’t say I have episodes where I’m eating a huge amount all at once and can’t stop, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSometimes I get confused with that question, because what feels like “a lot” to me might not actually be a lot to someone else. I can feel really uncomfortable even after what other people would probably consider a normal amount, so in my head it can feel like I’ve overeaten. But if you mean objectively, like a large amount of food in a short period, then no, I don’t regularly do that.\n\nI’m more likely to be thinking too much about what I’ve had than eating large amounts quickly, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed myself eating much faster, no.

When I feel like I’ve overeaten, it’s usually more that I’m suddenly very aware of what I’ve eaten and I start going over it in my head, like replaying it and thinking, “That was too much,” even if I probably wasn’t eating quickly. I might feel rushed inside, if that makes sense, like mentally panicked, but I don’t know that the actual eating is fast.

I suppose there might be times where I’m a bit more tense or distracted and I don’t remember every bite clearly, but I wouldn’t say I’m shoveling food down or eating much more quickly than usual. It’s more the feeling afterward that’s overwhelming than the speed of it.

Like if I eat more than I planned, or if I finish something I only meant to have a bite of, my brain immediately labels it as too much.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there are times where I eat a really large amount in a short period, like… I don’t think so? Even a normal portion can feel excessive to me afterward, and then I replay it and get stuck on it.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed myself eating a large amount all at once, but I do feel like I’ve overdone it even when it might not look that way from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. I usually feel like I’m trying very hard to stay in control the whole time, almost too much, honestly.
